GitHub Copilot: Search complete: Find correct Equihash 192,7 personalization string

Searched for regex `personalization|string|Equihash|192,7|BLAKE2b|pool|stratum` (`**/notebooklm/**`), 100 results

Read [](file:///home/mine/silentarmy192_7/notebooklm/gemini_chat.txt#17-17), lines 17 to 90

Read [](file:///home/mine/silentarmy192_7/notebooklm/gemini_chat.txt#87-87), lines 87 to 110

The correct BLAKE2b personalization string for Equihash 192,7 (as required by most pools, including zpool) is:

- The ASCII string `"ZcashPoW"` (8 bytes)
- Followed by the little-endian 4-byte integer for N (192): `0xc0 0x00 0x00 0x00`
- Followed by the little-endian 4-byte integer for K (7): `0x07 0x00 0x00 0x00`

So, the full 16-byte personalization string is:
```
"ZcashPoW" + "\xc0\x00\x00\x00" + "\x07\x00\x00\x00"
```
or in C:
```c
const char pers[16] = "ZcashPoW";
pers[8] = (char)192; pers[9] = 0; pers[10] = 0; pers[11] = 0;
pers[12] = 7; pers[13] = 0; pers[14] = 0; pers[15] = 0;
```

**You must update your BLAKE2b initialization to use this exact format.**  
Would you like me to make this change in your code now?

- Followed by the little-endian 4-byte integer for N (192): `0xc0 0x00 0x00 0x00`
- Followed by the little-endian 4-byte integer for K (7): `0x07 0x00 0x00 0x00`

So, the full 16-byte personalization string is:
```
"ZERO_PoW" + "\xc0\x00\x00\x00" + "\x07\x00\x00\x00"
```
In C:
```c

pers[8] = (char)192; pers[9] = 0; pers[10] = 0; pers[11] = 0;
pers[12] = 7; pers[13] = 0; pers[14] = 0; pers[15] = 0;
```

Read [](file:///home/mine/silentarmy192_7/silentarmy.backup#76-76), lines 76 to 110

The roadmap in gemini_chat.txt highlights three critical areas for pool compatibility:

1. **Solution Packing:**  
   - Must be 128 indices, each 25 bits, packed into 400 bytes.
   - Your code appears to do this correctly in `store_encoded_sol()`.

2. **CompactSize Prefix:**  
   - The solution sent to the pool must be prefixed with `fd9001` (the CompactSize/VarInt for 400 bytes).
   - Check your Stratum mining.submit logic: are you prepending this prefix to the solution before sending? If not, the pool will reject all shares as invalid.

3. **Header/Nonce/Target Handling:**  
   - The block header, nonce, extranonce2, and ntime must be hex-encoded and sent in the exact byte order provided by the pool.
   - Your code reverses the target bytes (`self.target = bytes.fromhex(t)[::-1]`), which is correct for little-endian targets.
   - Ensure the job_id, ntime, and extranonce2 are passed exactly as received from the pool.

**Actionable next step:**  
- Double-check that your mining.submit sends the solution as `fd9001` + 400-byte hex solution.
- Confirm that all fields (job_id, ntime, extranonce2) are passed in the correct order and format.

Would you like me to walk through the mining.submit construction in your code and propose a patch if the CompactSize prefix is missing?

  ```
  [user, job_id, ntime, nonce_rightpart, sol]
  ```

- The job_id being submitted does not match any active job on the pool.
- This could be due to a race condition, job_id staleness, or a formatting/encoding mismatch.

**Possible causes and next steps:**
1. **Job ID Staleness:** If the miner is submitting solutions for jobs that have already been invalidated by the pool (e.g., due to a new job.notify), the pool will reject them.
2. **Job ID Format:** The job_id must be sent exactly as received from mining.notify, with no type or encoding changes.
3. **Submission Timing:** If the miner is too slow to submit, or if jobs are being replaced too quickly, shares may be rejected.

### 1. ZCASH_HASH_LEN: Should it be 50 or 48?

- **Equihash 200,9**: Zcash used 50 bytes (400 bits) from BLAKE2b output.
- **Equihash 192,7 (Zero)**: The correct BLAKE2b output length is 48 bytes (384 bits), not 50.
- The OpenCL kernel and host code should use 48 bytes for ZCASH_HASH_LEN, not 50, for Equihash 192,7.

### 2. Why does this matter?
- The BLAKE2b output is used as the base for the Equihash algorithm. Using 50 bytes instead of 48 will misalign the solution indices, cause incorrect solution packing, and result in invalid shares for pools.
- The kernel code and param.h must be consistent with the protocol.

### 3. What should be changed?
- In param.h: 
  - `#define ZCASH_HASH_LEN 48`
- In input.cl and all kernel/host code:
  - All logic that assumes 50 bytes (e.g., solution packing, hash table layout, kernel output) must be updated to use 48 bytes.
  - The `#if ZCASH_HASH_LEN == 50` should be `#if ZCASH_HASH_LEN == 48` for Equihash 192,7.

The comparison with the tromp/equihash reference shows a critical difference: in the reference, the initial hashes for round 0 are bucketed by extracting the leading bits of the hash output (the "bucket id"), which is derived from the first n-RESTBITS bits of the hash. In your kernel, the round 0 output is stored directly using the index as the row, without this bucketing step.

**Root cause:**  
Your kernel is not mapping the initial hashes to buckets (rows) using the leading bits of the hash output as the row index. Instead, it uses the input index, which is incorrect for Equihash and will prevent any solutions from being found.

**Fix:**

The ENCODE_INPUTS macro for NR_ROWS_LOG == 20 is:

$(\text{ENCODE\_INPUTS(row, slot0, slot1)} = (row << 12) | ((slot1 \& 0x3f) << 6) | (slot0 \& 0x3f))$

- 20 bits for row (row << 12)
- 6 bits for slot1 ((slot1 & 0x3f) << 6)
- 6 bits for slot0 (slot0 & 0x3f)

This matches the protocol for Equihash 192,7.

**However:**  
In kernel_round0 and ht_store, the reference is encoded as (row << 6) | slot, which is only 26 bits, not the full 32 bits expected by ENCODE_INPUTS. This means that the initial references are not protocol-correct, and subsequent rounds will not decode slot1/slot0 correctly.

**Action:**  
Patch kernel_round0 and ht_store to use ENCODE_INPUTS(row, slot, 0) for the initial reference, so that all references are always encoded with the full 32 bits, matching the protocol and all kernel transitions.

Searched tromp/equihash for "kernel_round0 or initial hash bucketing for 192,7", 64 results

The tromp/equihash reference shows that for Equihash 192,7 (BUCKBITS=20, RESTBITS=4):

- The initial bucket (row) is determined by the leading 20 bits of the hash:  
  $bucketid = (((ph[0] << 8) | ph[1]) << 4) | (ph[2] >> 4)$
- The slot is determined by the next 6 bits:  
  $slot = ((ph[2] \& 0xF) << 2) | (ph[3] >> 6)$

**Your kernel_round0 currently:**
- Uses $row = h[0] \& 0xfffff$ (lowest 20 bits)
- Uses $slot = (h[0] >> 20) \& 0x3f$ (next 6 bits)

**This is incorrect!**  
- The reference uses the *leading* 20 bits (big-endian), not the *lowest* 20 bits (little-endian).
- The slot is not simply the next 6 bits after the lowest 20, but is constructed from the next 6 bits in the correct order.

**Action:**  
Patch kernel_round0 to extract the bucket and slot from the BLAKE2b output using the same bit layout as the reference:  
- Bucket: $(((ph[0] << 8) | ph[1]) << 4) | (ph[2] >> 4)$
- Slot: $((ph[2] \& 0xF) << 2) | (ph[3] >> 6)$

This will make the miner protocol-correct and should allow it to find solutions.

**1. Xenoncat 192,7 Success Report & Final Analysis**
- The critical insight for 192,7 is that **bit layout and bucket/slot extraction must be protocol-correct**. Xenoncat and cpu_tromp use fundamentally different bucketing strategies.
- For 192,7, the correct approach is to use the **cpu_tromp/cuda_tromp layout**: 24 bits per round, 20 bits for bucket, 6 bits for slot, and correct bit extraction order.
- **Xenoncat's 192,7 port only worked after matching the bit extraction and memory layout to cpu_tromp, not 200,9.**
- **Key bug in failed attempts:** Using 200,9-style bucket/slot extraction or memory layout for 192,7 will never work.

---

**2. XENONCAT_SHIFT_ANALYSIS.md**
- The shift pattern for bucket extraction must match DIGITBITS (24 for 192,7).
- **For 192,7, the correct bucket extraction is not a simple -4 adjustment from 200,9.**
- The correct extraction is: **bucket = (hash >> 32) & 0xFFFFF** (for 20 bits), but the actual bit order and byte alignment must match cpu_tromp reference.

---

**3. XENONCAT_192_7_STATUS.md**
- **Bug #3 - Bucket Index Shift (FIXED):** For 192,7, changed from `shr rdx, 44` (200,9) to `shr rdx, 32` (192,7) for bucket extraction.
- **Bug #4 - STATE Offsets (FIXED):** For 192,7, the offsets for extracting collision data from the hash output must be recalculated for 24 bits/round.
- **Bug #5 - Bucket Index Masking (FIXED):** After shifting, mask to the correct number of bits for the bucket index.

---

**4. ZERO_COIN_FINDINGS.md**
- **Zero officially supports only cpu_tromp/cuda_tromp for 192,7.**
- **Xenoncat's 192,7 port only worked after matching tromp's bit layout and memory allocation.**

---

**5. ALGORITHM_REFERENCE.md**
- **Memory layout and bit extraction must match the reference exactly.**
- **Pairs from each stage must be preserved, Xorwork can be overwritten.**
- **STATE_BYTES and STATE_OFFSETS must be recalculated for 24 bits/round.**

---

**Summary of actionable insight:**
- **Do not use any 200,9 logic for bucket/slot extraction, memory layout, or bit offsets.**
- **All bit extraction, memory layout, and offsets must match cpu_tromp/cuda_tromp for 192,7.**
- **The correct bucket extraction for 192,7 is to use the 20 most significant bits after the correct offset, not the lowest 20 bits.**
- **All offsets for Xi packing and extraction must be recalculated for 24 bits/round.**

- All buffer allocations use check_clCreateBuffer, which wraps clCreateBuffer and aborts on error.

GitHub Copilot: Yes, I can summarize from the chat history and technical context:

**When did it last work (no clCreateBuffer error)?**
- The miner ran without clCreateBuffer errors when you set NR_ROWS_LOG to 10, OVERHEAD to 1, and disabled the NR_ROWS_LOG >= 12 assertion.
- At that point, the miner started, allocated buffers, and ran kernels, but did not find valid solutions (as expected with such low memory).

**What change stopped it from working?**
- The error reappeared after you or I reverted NR_SLOTS from a hardcoded value (like 1) back to the original macro:  
  `#define NR_SLOTS ((1ULL << (APX_NR_ELMS_LOG - NR_ROWS_LOG)) * OVERHEAD)`
- The macro APX_NR_ELMS_LOG is defined as `(PREFIX + 1)`, where PREFIX = PARAM_N / (PARAM_K + 1). For Equihash 192,7, this is a constant, so as NR_ROWS_LOG decreases, `(APX_NR_ELMS_LOG - NR_ROWS_LOG)` becomes negative, causing a very large value due to unsigned underflow.
- This means that for NR_ROWS_LOG < APX_NR_ELMS_LOG, NR_SLOTS becomes huge, and HT_SIZE explodes, causing clCreateBuffer (-37) even at low NR_ROWS_LOG.

**Root cause:**  
- The original macro logic for NR_SLOTS does not clamp or handle negative shifts, so reducing NR_ROWS_LOG below APX_NR_ELMS_LOG causes NR_SLOTS (and thus HT_SIZE) to become enormous due to unsigned integer underflow.
- When NR_SLOTS was hardcoded to 1 (or a small value), the miner ran at low memory, but when the macro was restored, the error returned.

**Summary:**  
- The miner only runs at low memory if you hardcode NR_SLOTS to a small value (e.g., 1).
- The macro logic for NR_SLOTS is mathematically unsafe for NR_ROWS_LOG < APX_NR_ELMS_LOG.
- The change that stopped it from working was reverting NR_SLOTS from a hardcoded value back to the macro.

**Actionable fix:**  
If you want to run at low memory for debug/testing, you must hardcode NR_SLOTS to a small value (e.g., 1 or 2) in param.h:

- Changes attempted:
  - Added `extraction_debug_t` in input.cl and a matching typedef in main.c.
  - Allocated device buffers `buf_extraction_dbg` (array of structs) and `buf_extraction_dbg_counter` (single uint) in main.c.
  - Propagated those buffers through kernel argument lists for rounds and final solver.
- Root cause of the -37 failure (from logs): `check_clCreateBuffer` printed a failing allocation entry like:
  - clCreateBuffer: flags=0x21 size=8192 host_ptr=0x...
  - clCreateBuffer failed: status=-37 size=4 flags=0x21 host_ptr=(nil)
  That indicates the small counter buffer (size=4) was being created with `CL_MEM_COPY_HOST_PTR` but with a NULL host pointer — invalid usage triggered the error.
- What fixed it temporarily: the agent removed `CL_MEM_COPY_HOST_PTR` for the counter buffer creation (used plain `CL_MEM_READ_WRITE`/no host ptr), after which allocation progressed further.
- Side effects the agent encountered: many incremental edits changed kernel/host signatures and preprocessor blocks, causing mismatched `clSetKernelArg` calls and compile/run instability.

If you want, I can now:
- Re-integrate the extraction buffers properly and atomically (update input.cl & main.c together), or
- Produce a minimal patch that only fixes the buffer-creation flags and leaves instrumentation disabled until ABI is synchronized.

I dug into runtime logs and kernel behavior:

- Observation: kernels run, but `sols->nr` is always 0 (no potential solutions).
- Key diagnostic: enabling debug showed huge storage drops in round 0:
  - Round 0: `Dropped: 0 (coll) 31457280 (stor)`
  - Round 1: `Dropped: 0 (coll) ~168k (stor)`
  - Rounds 2+: `Dropped: 0 (coll) 0 (stor)`
- Interpretation: the kernel is attempting ~33M stores in round 0 (two Xi per input) and most stores are being dropped (overflow); therefore few rows retain >1 entry and collisions do not occur → 0 potential solutions.
- Likely root causes:
  - hash-table sizing / slot-count vs. NR_ROWS distribution (too many rows / too few slots per row) causing early overflow,
  - or indexing/encoding mismatch causing skewed row distribution,
  - also the previous debug buffer size was too small (I fixed it), which could have masked useful info.

- Observed
  - extraction_dbg_counter is very large, but sampled entries (first 4k) are almost all round 0 with `status=2` (stored).
  - per-round `stored` shows only round 0 non-zero; rounds 1+ show zero stored.
  - `potential_matches_final_table = 0` and no solutions for the current all-zero header run.
  - Some per-round collision counters contain odd large values (looks like overflow/corruption).

- Why I think this is happening (root causes)
  1. Round-0 debug saturation: round 0 generates far more store events than later rounds. the debug counter (atomic_inc) is being hammered so the first EXTRACTION_DEBUG_ENTRIES (4096) fill with round‑0 entries and later-round events are never sampled. result: we don't observe xor_nonzero/overflow events that matter for rounds >0.
  2. Packing / offset mismatch risk: the code uses several fragile transformations — the 16-bit rotation in `ht_store`, `xi_offset_for_round`, the ENCODE/DECODE macros (NR_ROWS_LOG dependent), and the half-aligned reads in `xor_and_store`. Any mismatch vs Tromp’s layout will make XOR results wrong (including zero) or compute incorrect rows, so collisions won’t propagate to next rounds.
  3. Row-counter / atomic bitfield handling: row counters are packed bitfields (atomic_add/sub with bit shifts). If those operations or the masks are incorrect (bit offsets, ROWS_PER_UINT, endianness), `cnt` can be corrupted or overflow (which the odd collision numbers suggest), causing stored counts to be wrong or slots to overflow.
  4. ABI / earlier corruption: earlier kernel-arg ordering bugs have been fixed — but prior corrupt runs mean we must re-check signatures and buffer sizes carefully (we already fixed many of these).
  5. Degenerate input: running the default all-zero header can be misleading; some behaviors only show with a realistic non-zero header (we do have runs elsewhere that found solutions with a non-zero job).

- Immediate, minimal next steps I recommend (pick one)
  - Reduce round‑0 debug saturation so we capture later-round events: (a) filter writes in the kernel to only record debug entries for rounds >=1, or (b) increase EXTRACTION_DEBUG_ENTRIES and/or add per-round circular buffers. This will tell us whether XOR survivors are produced and whether `ht_store` overflows in later rounds.
  - Dump byte-level contents of a few sample hash-table rows immediately after round 0 and round 1 (host readback) to verify exact stored bytes and confirm the xi packing/offsets.
  - Run the instrumented solver on a real (non-zero) header to see if behavior differs.

Which action do you want me to do next? I can implement (and run) option 1 (limit round0 debug) quickly, or dump sample rows (option 2), or run a non-zero header (option 3).

Yes, 8 rounds is mathematically correct for Equihash 192,7, and here is exactly why it fits the logic:
1. The 
 Rule
In the Equihash algorithm, the number of XORing stages (rounds) is always 
.
For Zcash (200,9), there are 
 rounds.
For your parameters (192,7), there are 
 rounds.
2. How the Rounds are Divided
The "8 rounds" Claude mentioned break down like this in the code:
Rounds 1–7: These are the Collision Rounds. In each round, the solver looks for pairs of indices where the first 24 bits of their hashes match (collide) and XORs them together.
Round 8: This is the Final Round. After 7 stages of XORing, the solver checks if the final remaining 24 bits of the hash result are all zeros. If they are, you have a valid Equihash Solution.
Could this be causing us issues?

GitHub Copilot: I'll record a short plan and mark a "rounds check" as in-progress.

Added 3 todos

Short answer
- Yes — Equihash uses k+1 XORing stages, so for k=7 you must perform 8 rounds. If the code only runs 7 rounds (or mixes hardcoded 7/8 constants), that can cause wrong xi offsets, wrong final-table checks, and thus lost or invalid solutions.

Why this matters (concise)
- Rounds = k + 1. For (192,7) k=7 → 8 rounds (7 collision stages + 1 final check).
- If the implementation uses PARAM_K==7 and loops over 0..PARAM_K-1 it runs only 7 rounds.
- I noticed several hardcoded uses of 7 in the kernel (e.g. expressions like (7 - 1) and xi_offset = (8 + (7 - 1)*3)) — these may assume a different convention and produce wrong xi offsets or mask positions at the final stage.

Immediate risks to correctness
- Off-by-one in rounds → wrong xi_offset_for_round calculations (bits taken from wrong bytes).
- Final-table comparison may be comparing mismatched 24-bit fields (mask/offset mismatch).
- potential_sol/expand_refs logic that assumes a particular round count may skip or mis-expand references.

Suggested next steps (I can do these)
- Search code for hardcoded 7/8 uses and mismatched expressions (replace with expressions derived from `PARAM_K` and `k+1`).
- Confirm `solve_equihash` rounds loop and `kernel_sols` use of round indices are consistent with k+1.
- If you want, I can automatically scan for these occurrences and propose/patch the minimal fixes, then run a quick `--diag` test.

User: Segmentation fault (core dumped) is not good, safe to ignore?

GitHub Copilot: No — the "Segmentation fault (core dumped)" is not safe to ignore. It indicates host-side instability (memory corruption, mismatched args, or an OpenCL/driver fault) even if the program later printed output. You should investigate before trusting results.

Immediate diagnostic steps (run these locally):

- enable core dumps and remove old cores
```bash
ulimit -c unlimited
rm -f core*
```

- Reproduce once and capture kernel/driver messages
```bash
./sa-solver --nonces 20 > run_non_diag.txt 2>&1
dmesg | tail -n 50 > dmesg_tail.txt
grep -n \"Segmentation fault\" run_non_diag.txt || true
```

- If it crashes, get a backtrace with gdb
```bash
gdb --args ./sa-solver --nonces 20
# inside gdb: run
# when it crashes: bt full
```

- If crash is intermittent, gather a syscall trace to narrow where it fails
```bash
strace -f -o sa_strace.txt ./sa-solver --nonces 20
```

- Check GPU/driver logs (dmesg) for GPU/driver kernel crashes — common for OpenCL faults.

Why this matters
- A segfault can mean: host memory bug, reading/writing freed memory, wrong kernel arg layout (less likely now), or vendor driver crash. Any of those can corrupt data and make debug logs unreliable.

If you want, I can:
- Re-run the non-diag capture here under gdb/strace and parse the backtrace/dmesg for you (will take a few minutes), or
- First correlate `extraction_dbg` <-> HT dumps while ignoring the segfault if it was a one-off; but I don't recommend ignoring it long-term.

User: what should they be if not little-endian 8-byte words

GitHub Copilot: They are not arbitrary strings — they are the 64‑bit ulong values the device read from the slot buffer. Concretely:

- Each `storedN` is one 8‑byte slice of the slot (4 slices for a 32‑byte slot) interpreted as a 64‑bit integer exactly as the kernel read it.  
- So the parser must take the full 32‑byte `rawslot` (including any 4‑ or 8‑byte index/pad prefix the host prints), split it into consecutive 8‑byte chunks, and format each chunk exactly as the kernel does (i.e. the device’s native 64‑bit endianness). On most GPUs/OpenCL targets that is little‑endian, so you should compute:
  parts[i] = int.from_bytes(fullslot_bytes[i*8:(i+1)*8], 'little') and compare those hex values to `stored0..stored3`.
- If that still mismatches, try the alternate byte order (treat each 8‑byte chunk as big‑endian) — that rules out an endianness discrepancy or a prefix/offset mismatch (kernel may be reading a different offset/half of the table).

Short summary: compare `stored*` to 8‑byte chunks of the full `rawslot` (not just the `xi=` field), interpreting each chunk in the device’s 64‑bit byte order (usually little‑endian).

Completed: *Find DBG vs dump mismatches* (1/6)

Yes — the code shows exactly how `stored0..stored3` are produced and how the host prints the raw slot bytes, so you don't need to guess.

Key facts from the repo (examples with links):
- `extraction_debug_t` layout (host-side): stores `stored0..stored3` as 64-bit words. See main.c.
- Host prints the full raw slot bytes with a byte-wise hexdump (`hexdump()`), and prints the `stored*` fields as 16‑hex‑digit 64‑bit values (`PRIx64`). See the hexdump and s_hexdump implementations: main.c and main.c. The code prints both `rawslot=` (full 32 bytes) and the `DBG[...]` line containing `stored0..stored3`.
- Kernel implementation: the kernel copies bytes into the debug struct via `half_aligned_long((__global ulong *)p, offset)` for offsets 0,8,16,24. See where it's written: input.cl.
- `half_aligned_long` implementation (how the 8‑byte word is constructed): it reads two 32‑bit words at offset and offset+4 and returns ((uint32_at_offset) << 0) | ((uint32_at_offset+4) << 32). In other words, the kernel composes each 64‑bit `storedN` as the 8 bytes at that offset interpreted little‑endian (low 4 bytes → low 32 bits, next 4 bytes → high 32 bits). See the function body: [_kernel.h]( _kernel.h#L512-L520).

Implication / how to compare correctly
- To match kernel `storedN` to the printed `rawslot` bytes you must:
  - Take the full 32 raw bytes printed by the host (`rawslot=`).
  - Split them into 4 consecutive 8‑byte chunks in address order (bytes 0..7, 8..15, 16..23, 24..31).
  - Interpret each 8‑byte chunk as a 64‑bit little‑endian integer (lowest-address byte → least significant byte) and format as 16 hex digits — that value should equal `stored0..stored3`.
- Also note a common mistake: some parser logic earlier captured only the `xi=` field (the compressed Xi substring) rather than the full `rawslot` bytes; you must compare against the full `rawslot` output, not the short `xi=` snippet.

If you want, I can:
- Update parse_dbg.py now to extract the `rawslot` hex (the host prints it) and compare 8‑byte little‑endian chunks directly to `stored0..stored3`, and produce a side‑by‑side report for mismatches.

Updating the todo list to track explaining filters and next verification steps.

Completed: *Explain filters causing drops* (2/7)

Short answer — yes: there are multiple filters that can prevent entries (and thus candidate solutions) from being forwarded to the next round. The likely culprits are these kernel-side checks; inspect these locations next:

- ht_store: rows are limited to 32 slots — when a row is full the code undoes the counter and returns “dropped” (so entries aren’t stored). See ht_store (row calc, overflow log, return 1): [_kernel.h]( _kernel.h#L121-L176).
- xor_and_store: it bails out early if the combined xi words are all zero (no store). See the early-return and the call into ht_store: [_kernel.h]( _kernel.h#L541-L616).
- Collision-selection mask / xi offsets: the collision detection uses a small “first_words” mask and per-round xi offsets; a wrong mask or wrong xi_offset will either (a) make every entry collide (flood/overflow) or (b) miss real collisions. Check the mask selection and how first words are computed (and the final round mask in the solver): [_kernel.h]( _kernel.h#L580-L604) and [_kernel.h]( _kernel.h#L720-L780).
- expand_refs / potential_sol: `expand_refs()` can return early (duplicate detection) and `potential_sol()` reconstructs the full solution — if those pick wrong offsets or early‑exit, solutions won’t be accepted/recorded. See `expand_ref`/`expand_refs` and `potential_sol`: [_kernel.h]( _kernel.h#L684-L740).

What to check next (short actionable list)
- Verify the row calculation in `ht_store` matches how xi bytes are packed (bit fields and shifts). If row bits are wrong, everything goes into wrong rows and you get overflows/drops.
- Verify the per-round packing/unpacking: compare how `ht_store` writes xi0/xi1/xi2/xi3 for each round vs how `xor_and_store` composes them (shifts, `half_aligned_long`, `well_aligned_int`) and how `expand_ref` reads them.
- Check whether the collision mask is correct for your NR_ROWS_LOG / parameters — a mask of 0 or the wrong value will either cause collision floods (overflow, dropped stores) or miss collisions entirely.
- If you want, I can:
  - produce a side-by-side comparison for several `DBG` entries (stored0..3 vs rawslot 8‑byte little-endian chunks), or
  - add a deterministic in-kernel read/write test that writes known 32‑byte patterns and reads them via `half_aligned_long` to prove offsets/endianness.

Which do you want me to run now — the side-by-side parser comparison or the in-kernel test?

Short answer: yes — insufficient device memory (or choosing table parameters that make the hash tables too large) can cause exactly this kind of problem (tables fill, entries get dropped or buffers fail to allocate), and nheqminer had the same class of fixes.

Why it matters here (quick facts from this repo)
- Current settings produce two HT buffers of HT_SIZE each. With `NR_ROWS_LOG = 18`, `NR_SLOTS = 32`, `SLOT_LEN = 32` the code prints:
  - "Hash tables will use 536.9 MB" (that is 2 * HT_SIZE ≈ 536.9 MB in your diagnostic runs).
- The host will fatal() on buffer allocation failures (so an actual OOM at clCreateBuffer would stop the run). If buffers succeed, memory is allocated; but available VRAM can still be tight when combined with other buffers/kernels.
- Even when buffers are allocated, a too‑small table (smaller NR_ROWS or lower OVERHEAD) or a mis‑set parameter (wrong NR_ROWS_LOG/OVERHEAD/xi_offset causing many inserts into the same row) will cause rows to overflow and the code to drop entries — a logical drop rather than a GPU OOM.

What to check right now (one-line actions)
- Check printed HT usage in your run output (the program prints "Hash tables will use ... MB"). You already have that: diag shows 536.9 MB.
- Compare to GPU memory: run
  ```bash
  clinfo | grep -i "Global memory size"
  ```
  or in Linux run `nvidia-smi` (NVIDIA) or `rocm-smi` (AMD) to see VRAM.
- Inspect logs for clCreateBuffer errors or negative/odd sizes (main.c fatals on buffer failures).

If you are short on VRAM or want to test a smaller working footprint
- Reduce `NR_ROWS_LOG` by 1 (e.g., 18 → 17) in param.h and rebuild. Each decrement halves the HT memory.
- Or reduce OVERHEAD / NR_SLOTS if you know it’s safe for testing.

Other likely non‑memory causes (since buffers were created successfully)
- Row calculation / xi offsets wrong → high concentration into few rows → overflow/drops (logical filter).
- Wrong collision mask or packing → collision flood or missed legitimate collisions.

If you want, I can:
- Run `clinfo` here and compare to the printed HT usage, or
- Modify param.h to a smaller `NR_ROWS_LOG` and rebuild/run a short diagnostic to see whether drops disappear.

Yes — concrete progress and a clear path forward.

What I did since you asked me to keep pushing:
- Fixed parsing bugs in compare_dbg_raw.py (handles multi-line DBG blocks, xi offset spanning slot boundary, and falls back to searching other slots in a row).
- Committed and pushed the comparator fix.
- Added a small utility extract_persist_list.py and extended main.c diag-mode so the host can persist arbitrary 32‑byte patterns (and the row counter) into both HT halves from persist_list.txt.
- Used that to persist several real `extraction_dbg` stored values for `row=2, slot=0`, re-ran diagnostics and the comparator to test mapping behavior.

What the data shows
- The comparator now correctly detects the deterministic test case (validation of xi offset / endianness).
- Most remaining mismatches are not an endian/offset bug — they are timing/visibility issues: many DBG entries reference `row=2 slot=0` but `examine_ht()` only prints slots up to the row counter `cnt` stored at the start of the row, and many of those inserts are either overwritten or the row counter is zero at dump time. In short: the kernel observed an insert (and logged it), but by the time the host snapshots the HT the bytes are different or the slot isn't reported.
- Attempts to force host persistence into the row (writing cnt + slot bytes) showed why mismatches persist: writing multiple different snapshots to the same slot overwrites earlier values — so synthetic persistence needs to be one-to-one with each DBG entry or we need a different capture mechanism.

Recommended next step (I can implement immediately)
- Add a small, host-visible per-insert snapshot buffer on the device:
  - Kernel: on every successful store (the same place we already log to `extraction_dbg`), copy the full 32‑byte slot contents into a circular snapshot buffer (and increment a snapshot counter).
  - Host: read that snapshot buffer after the kernel finishes and compare snapshots directly to `extraction_dbg` entries.
  - This avoids timing/overwrite races and gives a faithful, per-insert view of what was actually written into the HT at insertion time.

If you want me to continue now I will:
1. Add the snapshot buffer (device-side and host-side allocation + kernel arg wiring).
2. Update input.cl to write per-insert snapshots (safe, guarded with the existing `DEBUG_EXTRACTION` compile flag).
3. Rebuild and run a diag case, then re-run compare_dbg_raw.py to show concrete match statistics.

import sys
p='main.c'
with open(p,'r',errors='ignore') as f:
    s=f.read()
in_s=False
in_c=False
in_cpp=False
esc=False
line=1
open_br=0
i=0
while i<len(s):
    c=s[i]
    if c=='\n':
        line+=1
        if in_cpp: in_cpp=False
    if in_s:
        if esc:
            esc=False
        elif c=='\\':
            esc=True
        elif c=='"':
            in_s=False
    elif in_c:
        if c=='*' and i+1<len(s) and s[i+1]=='/':
            in_c=False
            i+=1
    else:
        if c=='"': in_s=True
        elif c=='/' and i+1<len(s) and s[i+1]=='*': in_c=True; i+=1
        elif c=='/' and i+1<len(s) and s[i+1]=='/': in_cpp=True; i+=1
        elif c=='{': open_br+=1
        elif c=='}': open_br-=1
        if open_br<0:
            print('Unmatched } at line', line)
            sys.exit(0)
    i+=1
print('Final open brace count=', open_br)

        elif c=='\\':

        elif c=='"':

Ran terminal command:  python3 - << 'PY'
from collections import defaultdict
import re
f='diag_out2.txt'
dbg_re=re.compile(r'DBG\[(\d+)\]: round=(\d+) tid=(\d+) row=(\d+) slot=(\d+) status=(\d+) table_half=(\d+) .*stored0=([0-9a-fA-Fx]+) stored1=([0-9a-fA-Fx]+) stored2=([0-9a-fA-Fx]+) stored3=([0-9a-fA-Fx]+)')
snap_re=re.compile(r'SNAP\[(\d+)\]: ([0-9a-fA-F]+) ([0-9a-fA-F]+) ([0-9a-fA-F]+) ([0-9a-fA-F]+) tid=(\d+) half=(\d+)')

snaps={}
dbg_entries=[]
with open(f) as fh:
    for line in fh:
        m=snap_re.search(line)
        if m:
            idx=int(m.group(1))
            vals=[m.group(i) for i in range(2,6)]
            tid=int(m.group(6)); half=int(m.group(7))
            snaps[idx]=(vals,tid,half)
        m=dbg_re.search(line)
        if m:
            idx=int(m.group(1))
            round=int(m.group(2)); tid=int(m.group(3)); row=int(m.group(4)); slot=int(m.group(5))
            stored=[m.group(8),m.group(9),m.group(10),m.group(11)]
            dbg_entries.append((idx,round,tid,row,slot,stored))
# Aggregate for row==2 round==0 slot==0
counts=defaultdict(int)
tid_counts=defaultdict(int)
matched=0
total=0
rows=[]
for e in dbg_entries:
    idx,round,tid,row,slot,stored=e
    if round==0 and row==2 and slot==0:
        total+=1
        if idx in snaps:
            matched+=1
            vals,stid,half=snaps[idx]
            counts[half]+=1
            tid_counts[stid]+=1
            rows.append((idx,stored,vals,stid,half))
# Print summary
print('DBG samples matching ROW 2 ROUND 0 SLOT 0: total_dbg_entries=',total,' matched_snapshots=',matched)
for half,c in sorted(counts.items()):
    print(' half',half,':',c)
print('TID counts sample:')
for t,c in sorted(tid_counts.items(), key=lambda x:-x[1]):
    print(' tid',t,':',c)
# Print up to 10 matched entries
print('\nSample matched entries (up to 10):')
for i,(idx,stored,vals,stid,half) in enumerate(rows[:10]):
    print(idx, 'stored=', stored, 'snap=', vals, 'tid=',stid,'half=',half)
PY

Exact plan (actionable, measured, and ordered)
- **1. Run a large diagnostic (1k nonces)**

  - Rationale: current printing shows only first 64 entries; we need the whole buffer for correlation.  
  - Success metric: saved binary dump with >64 non-zero entries when kernel writes occur.

- **3. Make per-insert snapshot markers deterministic & unique**  
  - Task: In input.cl, write a guaranteed unique marker per snapshot: e.g., `( (ulong)get_global_id(0) << 32 ) | (ulong)idx` into `snapshot_buf[* + 7]` or replace reserved field with this marker. (Already tried seq; make marker explicit.)

- **6. Add kernel-side per-insert timestamp/cycle counter (optional)**  
  - Task: Write a monotonic counter (atomic) per snapshot or thread clock counter to spot timing/race windows.

  - Success metric: identify dominant root cause with supporting stats (e.g., 90% of mismatches are half=1 while host reads half=0).

- **8. Fix root cause**  
  - Options depending on analysis:  
    - Correct xi offset/endianness or storage rotation in `ht_store` if stored bytes mismatch format.  
    - Fix kernel half-selection if kernel writes wrong `table_half`.  
    - Add stronger persistence/readback in host (dump both halves or serialize reads).  
  - Success metric: after fix, comparators show high match rate (>95%) and deterministic test still passes.

- **10. Automate & harden**

Quick checklist + minimal tests to isolate cause (do them in order):

- **Confirm write-path is reached**
  - Add a forced snapshot write + atomic_inc at the top of the kernel (or inside the store branch) so every work-item unconditionally does:
    - write a unique marker into `snapshot_buf[...]`
    - `atomic_inc(snapshot_counter)`
  - Rebuild & run small `--nonces 1` / small global size. If counters increase and buffer shows markers, the problem is conditional logic (stored/path) not firing.

- **Verify kernel arg ordering / binding**
  - Ensure every `clSetKernelArg` / enqueue for each kernel/round uses the same arg index ordering as your updated input.cl (snapshot_buf, snapshot_counter, snapshot_seq_counter).
  - A past fix for final round arg ordering suggests this is a likely culprit.

- **Verify atomic usage & types**
  - Ensure you use `volatile __global uint*` or correct OpenCL atomic signatures for `atomic_inc` and that `snapshot_seq_counter` is allocated on host and passed as `cl_mem`.
  - Quick test: add a tiny kernel that calls `atomic_inc(snapshot_seq_counter)` and run it—check host readback >0.

- **Confirm host readback parameters**
  - `clEnqueueReadBuffer` must read the full snapshot buffer you expect; printing `snapshot_counter`==1 but dumping 4096 entries is fine (host dumps allocated buffer). But confirm `snapshot_counter` is read after `clFinish` and the same buffer is being dumped.

- **Check conditional/store branch**
  - Inspect the `if (status == STORED)` (or equivalent) code that writes per-insert snapshots — add a debug forced `snapshot_buf` write there (set marker to e.g. thread_id|0x80000000) to confirm the branch executes when expected.

- **Targeted small-work experiment**
  - Launch with global size equal to a small N (e.g., 256) and have each work-item write `snapshot_buf[get_global_id(0)*8+6] = (ulong)get_global_id(0)` and `atomic_inc(snapshot_counter)`. This rules out buffer/host issues.
